In Kaliṅga are Subhadra and Śyāmādevī, pale yellow like the sky. In Lampāka are Vajrabhadra and Subhadrā, yellow in color. In Kāñcī are Mahābhairava and Hayakarṇā, green in color. In Himālaya are Virūpākṣa and Khagānanā, pale yellow like the sky. In Pretapurī are Mahābala and Cakravegā, yellow in color. In Gṛhadevatā are Ratnavajra and Khaṇḍarohā, green in color. In Saurāṣṭra are Hayagrīva and Śauṇḍinī, pale yellow like the sky. In Suvarṇadvīpa are Ākāśagarbha and Cakravarmiṇī, yellow in color. In Nagara are Heruka and Suvīrā, green in color. In Sindhu are Padmanarteśvara and Mahābalā, pale yellow like the sky. In Maru are Vairocana and Cakravartinī, yellow in color. In Kulatā are Vajrasattva and Mahāvīryā, green in color.

Or else, for body, speech, and mind,
One may distinguish the colors as one wishes.
Following the leftward order,
The gates are to be arranged.

In the east is Kākāsyā, in the north Ulūkāsyā, in the west Śvānāsyā, and in the south Sūkarāsyā; they are blue, green, red, and yellow in color. In the direction of Fire, in the southeast, is Yamadāhī, red in color. In the direction of Nairṛti, in the southwest, is Yamadūtī, blue in color. In the direction of Wind, in the northwest, is Yamadaṃṣṭrī, the color of smoke or of a rain cloud. In the direction of Īśāna, in the northeast, is Yamamathanī, white or pale yellow in color.

The heroes Khaṇḍakapāla and the rest, and Pracaṇḍā and the rest, are alike: they are one-faced and four-armed, abiding with the right leg extended, wearing a garland of skulls and clad in a tiger skin. They bear the five mudrās and are embraced by the legs of the goddesses. Naked, they are adorned with a garland of heads; they are three-eyed, their foreheads adorned with a garland of skulls. They embrace with vajra and bell, endowed with the sameness of nondual union, pressing the breasts of the goddesses. They hold a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga and bear a ḍamaru; or else, by the division into the three families, they hold the root emblem.

All are clad in the directions,
And the goddesses, with loose hair, are fierce.
The goddesses Kākāsyā and the rest,
With loose hair and clad in the directions,
Abide in the stance with the right leg extended.
Three-eyed, with distorted faces,
Wearing crowns of garlands of skulls,
Their braids adorned with garlands of heads,
All of them also bear the five mudrās.
Utterly intoxicated with desirous passion,
Together with their own method,
Unwavering, they are joined in the same way.
Bellies hanging and arms hanging,
Their bodies terrifying and faces distorted,
Utterly terrifying, upon a sun and a lotus,
They abide firmly for the accomplishment.
Just as anyone may wish,
Their faces impassioned:
With such a conception
The yogin should meditate.
They hold a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga,
And bear a ḍamaru, a curved knife, and a head.
All the maṇḍalas, every one of them,
Should be meditated on as seated upon pretas.
In the maṇḍala of the hosts of ḍākinīs,
After the close approach comes the yoga;
Through the union of the hero's limbs
One enters the absorption of the higher accomplishment.

The nails and teeth are Khaṇḍakapāla. The hair of the head and of the body is Mahākaṅkāla. The skin and its grime are Kaṅkāla. The flesh is Vikaṭadaṃṣṭrin. The sinews are Suravairin. The bones are Amitābha. The kidneys are Vajraprabha. The heart is Vajradeha. The eyes are Vajra Aṅkurika. The bile is Vajrajaṭila. The lungs are Mahāvīra. The intestines are Vajrahūṃkāra. The colon is Subhadra. The navel is Vajrabhadra. The bladder is Mahābhairava. The hairline is Virūpākṣa. The phlegm is Mahābala. The pus is Ratnavajra. The blood is Hayagrīva. The menstrual fluid is Ākāśagarbha. The fat is Śrī Heruka. The tears are Padmanarteśvara. The saliva is Vairocana. The mucus is Vajrasattva.

Pullīramalaya: on the head is Pracaṇḍā. Jālandhara: on the crown is Caṇḍākṣī. Oḍiyāna: in the right ear is Prabhāvatī. Arbuda: at the back of the head is Mahānāsā. These are the sacred places (pīṭha). Godāvarī: in the left ear is Vīramatī. Rāmeśvara: between the eyebrows is Kharvarī. Devīkoṭa: in the eyes is Laṅkeśvarī. Mālava: on the two shoulders is Drumacchāyā. These are the secondary sacred places. Kāmarūpa: in the armpits is Airāvatī. Oḍra: on the two breasts is Mahābhairavā. These are the fields (kṣetra). Triśakuni: at the navel is Vāyuvegā. Kosala: at the tip of the nose is Surābhakṣī. These are the secondary fields. Kaliṅga: in the mouth is Śyāmādevī. Lampāka: at the throat is Subhadrā. These are the meeting places (chandoha). Kāñcī: at the heart is Hayakarṇā. Himālaya: at the genital region is Khagānanā. These are the secondary meeting places. Pretapurī: at the sexual organ is Cakravegā. Gṛhadevatā: at the anus is Khaṇḍarohā. These are meeting places. Saurāṣṭra: on the two thighs is Śauṇḍinī. Suvarṇadvīpa: on the two calves is Cakravarmiṇī. These are secondary meeting places. Nagara: on the toes is Suvīrā. Sindhu: on the soles of the feet is Mahābalā. These are the charnel grounds. Maru: on the big toes is Cakravartinī. Kulatā: on the two knees is Mahāvīryā. These are the secondary charnel grounds.

The earth element is Pātanī. The water element is Māraṇī. The fire element is Ākarṣaṇī. The wind element is Narteśvarī. The space element is Padmajālinī. The aggregate of form is Vairocana. The aggregate of feeling is Vajrasūrya. The aggregate of perception is Padmanarteśvara. The aggregate of formations is Vajrarāja. The aggregate of consciousness is Vajrasattva, and the embodiment of all the tathāgatas is Śrī Heruka. The two eyes are Mohavajra. The two ears are Dveṣavajra. The two nostrils are Mātsaryavajra. The mouth is Rāgavajra. The tangible is Īrṣyāvajra. All the sense sources are Aiśvaryavajra. The mind is Akṣobhyavajra. Speech is Amitavajra. The body is Vajravairocana.

These are the yoga of accomplishment.
Through the application of the hook of one's own heart,
Having summoned the wheel of wisdom,
Having emanated and arranged them,
One also confers empowerment in the due sequence.
Once they have entered, one should bind them,
And unite with delight and with passion.
Having become one with one's own nature,
One sets the two armors upon it.

oṃ ha at the heart: Vajrasattva. namaḥ hi at the head: Vairocana. svāhā hu at the crown: Padmanarteśvara. vauṣaṭ he at the two shoulders: Heruka. hūṃ hūṃ hoḥmig at the two eyes: Vajrasūrya. phaṭ haṃ, the armor of the whole body: Paramāśva. oṃ vaṃ at the navel: Vajravārāhī. haṃ yaṃ at the heart: Yāminī. hrīṃ maṃ at the mouth: Mohanī. hreṃ hreṃ at the head: Sañcālinī. hūṃ hūṃ at the crown: Santrāsanī. phaṭ phaṭ, Caṇḍikā: the weapon of all the limbs.

Knotting the index fingers, one firmly joins the middle fingers,
And joins the thumbs firmly as a vajra.
Having placed this at the center of the forehead,
One should apply it, turning it round and round.
With the feet half pressed down, looking upward,
Proclaiming the sound pheṃ upward,
One summons the heroes and yoginīs
Who dwell in the world systems of the ten directions.
jaḥ hūṃ vaṃ hoḥ
Having summoned them through this application, one makes them enter,
Binds them, and brings them under control.
Having seen the heroes and yoginīs
Completely filling the center of the sky,
And having taken up a vase filled
With the nectar of the water of wisdom,
Empowerment is to be conferred upon him with their hands.
With offerings of song and dance
The empowerments are to be offered.
The mantra for this is oṃ sarvatathāgata abhiṣekata samaya śriye hūṃ hūṃ.

By singing this song they are urged on, or else the great ḍākinīs become pleased and delighted:

Great bliss of the music of empowerment,
Great bliss of the net of ḍākinīs,
Great bliss of offering great delight,
Great bliss of the net of ḍākinīs,
Illuminating great bliss,
Great bliss of the net of ḍākinīs,
a la la hoḥ
Great bliss that has become supreme,
Great bliss of the net of ḍākinīs,
a la la hoḥ

Having delighted in the wisdom of great bliss,
The commitment abides in the one who holds the commitment.
Having meditated on wisdom as of one taste,
One meditates likewise on the wheel of one taste.
Crowned with Vajrasattva,
Fully endowed with the five wisdoms,
Whatever heroes there are, the root mantra and the rest,
Should be conceived as the brahmin's thread and so forth.
Whatever is the mantra of Vajravārāhī
Adorns one as a necklace, from neck to neck.
With the heart mantra of the goddess
One is adorned with a garland of fifty,
And with the seven-syllable secret mantra
The jewel earrings are beautifully lovely.
The ḍākinī of all the buddhas
Is the supreme wearing of the skin garment.
With the secret mantras of the six yoginīs
The girdle is made into an ornament.
With the six armors
The body of the mudrā is beautifully adorned.
The mantra of the yoginīs of all the wheels
Is the skull cup for drinking.
Whatever mantra belongs to Vairocanī
Makes the skull an ornament on the crown.
With the twenty-four heroes
One should conceive the elements of the body.
Having thus contemplated oneself,
One guards well with the boundary or with mantra.
Having examined the conduct of celibacy,
One worships with the eight-line mantra.
Having abandoned every fear,
One contemplates oneself thus as the yogin.
One meditates on the wisdom of emanation and withdrawal,
And performs the recitation of the wisdom of union.
The great yoga, the great accomplishment:
From the drop of the vajra wrathful one
One should emanate into the three realms.
The tip of the vajra nose blazes.
To emanate, from the drop in space
A subtle vajra is seen,
And from between the pores of the body hair,
Into the moving and the unmoving,
One emanates clouds of wrathful buddhas.
The yoga of complete purification
Is the sequence of the thirty-seven.

The application of mindfulness to the body is the Ḍākinī. The application of mindfulness to feelings is Lāmā. The application of mindfulness to phenomena is Khaṇḍarohā. The application of mindfulness to the mind is Rūpiṇī. The basis of miraculous power of aspiration is Pracaṇḍā. The basis of miraculous power of diligence is Caṇḍākṣī. The basis of miraculous power of analysis is Prabhāvatī. The basis of miraculous power of mind is Mahānāsā. The faculty of faith is Vīramatī. The faculty of diligence is Kharvarī. The faculty of mindfulness is Laṅkeśvarī. The faculty of meditative absorption is Drumacchāyā. The faculty of wisdom is Airāvatī. The power of faith is Mahābhairavā. The power of diligence is Vāyuvegā. The power of mindfulness is Surābhakṣī. The power of meditative absorption is Śyāmādevī. The power of wisdom is Subhadrā. The limb of awakening that is meditative absorption is Hayakarṇā. The limb of awakening that is diligence is Khagānanā. The limb of awakening that is joy is Cakravegā. The limb of awakening that is pliancy is Khaṇḍarohā. The limb of awakening that is discrimination of phenomena is Śauṇḍinī. The limb of awakening that is mindfulness is Cakravarmiṇī. The limb of awakening that is equanimity is Suvīrā. Right view is Mahābalā. Right thought is Cakravartinī. Right speech is Mahāvīryā. Right action is Kākāsyā. Right livelihood is Ulūkāsyā. Right effort is Śvānāsyā. Right mindfulness is Sūkarāsyā. Right meditative absorption is the Blessed One, Śrī Heruka. Generating the virtuous phenomena that have not arisen is Yamadāhī. Fully protecting the virtuous phenomena that have arisen is Yamadūtī. Fully abandoning the nonvirtuous phenomena that have arisen is Yamadaṃṣṭrī. Not generating the nonvirtuous phenomena that have not arisen is Yamamathanī. This is the meditation that purifies the wheel of commitment.

Then the wheels of commitment,
Placed in equipoise, should recite:

ha ha hūṃ hūṃ hi hi phaṭ hūṃ hūṃ a a oṃ śrīvajra he he ru ru kaṃ hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ ḍākinījāla sambaraṃ hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā oṃ hrīḥ h ha hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ These are the heart and near-heart mantras.

oṃ vajravairocanīye hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā oṃ sarvabuddhaḍākinīye vajravarṇaṇ hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā These are the heart and near-heart mantras of the goddess.

oṃ ḍākini hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā oṃ lāme hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā oṃ khaṇḍarohe hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā oṃ rūbiṇi hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā oṃ khaṇḍakapālini kara kara hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā oṃ pracaṇḍe huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ mahākaṃkāla kuru kuru huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā oṃ caṇḍakṣi huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ kaṅkala bandha bandha huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ prabhavati huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vikaṭadāṃṣṭiṇi trāsaya trāsa huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ mahānāse huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ surāvairiṇi kṣobhaya kṣobhaya huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vīramati huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ amitābhe hrauṃ hrauṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ kharvari huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajraprabha hraḥ hraḥ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ laṅkeśvari huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajradehe pheṃ pheṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ drumacchāye huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ aṅkurike phaṭ phaṭ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ airāvati huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajrajaṭili daha daha huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ madābhairave huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ mahāvīra paca paca huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vāyuvege huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajrahūṃkāra bhakṣa bhakṣa vasarudhira antramālāvalambini huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ surabhakṣi huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ subhadre gṛhṇa gṛhṇa saptapātālagata bhūjiga sarvaṃ pātarjajya tarjaj huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ śyāmadevi huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajrabhadra ākaḍḍhya ākaḍḍhya huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ subhadre huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ mahābhairava hrīṃ hrīṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ hayakarṇeṇ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ virūpākṣa jṇauṃ jṇauṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ khagānane huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ mahābale kṣmāṃ kṣmāṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ cakravege huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ ratnavajra hāṃ hāṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ khaṇḍarohe huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ hayagrīva hīṃ hīṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ śauṇḍini huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ ākāśagarbhe hūṃ hūṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ cakravarmiṇi huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ heruka kili kili huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ suravīre huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ padmanarteśvare sili sili huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ mahābale huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vairocani hili hili huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ cakravartini huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajrasatva dhili dhili huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ mahāvīrye huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ kākāsye huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ ulūkāsye huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ śvānāsye huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ sūkarāsye huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ yamadāḍhi huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ yamadūti huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ yamadaṃṣṭri huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ yamamathani huṃ hūṃ phaṭ

In the due order of these, Having taken up a rosary of conch shell, With the thumb and ring finger of the left hand One should recite the secret mantras. At the four times, just as one wishes, The yogin should perform the recitation. Meditation is to be done one-pointedly, Joining commitment with commitment. Having concluded eating and recitation, One gives the torma according to the rite. At the maṇḍala, at one's ease, Seated naked with hair loose, The one who keeps the observance gives the torma. Standing with the right leg extended, In equipoise one binds the mudrā: Making the index fingers a spear, one firmly joins the middle fingers, And joins the thumbs firmly as a vajra. Having placed it in the region of the center of the forehead, Twisting and twisting it, turning it round, one says this: oṃ āralli hoḥ jaḥ hūṃ vaṃ hoḥ vajraḍākinī samaya stvaṃ dṛśya hoḥ This should be recited Three, four, or up to five times. Having become naked with hair loose, The yogin rises from a high seat, And standing facing the south, Gives the torma at midnight. Following whatever the rite may be, Having praised with the three realities, And opening the vajra palms upward, One proclaims these secret mantras: oṃ kha kha khāhi khāhi sarva yakṣa rākṣasa bhruta preta piśāca unmāda apasmāra ḍākaḍākinyadayaḥ imaṃ baliṃ ghṛhṇaṃtu samaya rakṣantu mama sarva siddhimme prayacchantu yathaivaṃ bhuñjatha vipatha jighratha mātikramatha mama sarvakāratāya sadsukhaṃ viśuddhaye sahāyikā bhavantu huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā

All the heroes and yoginīs,
Dancing with the sign of great bliss,
Abide in the body, speech, and mind of the Blessed One.
Having made the vowels and consonants equal,
There, through the application of the repha,
The repha blazing above
Causes dripping below.
That which has the nature of the dripping fluid
Becomes the form of deathless drops.
Churning and accomplishing are rightly joined.
Through the karmic wind
Fully stirring the fire of wisdom,
Smoke arises at the maṇḍala of the navel,
And rays of light are made to blaze.

That wheel of commitment, having burned all the tathāgatas, goes to the wheel of enjoyment of all the tathāgatas. By that method it circles three times, passes out through the door that opens the vital point at the hair curl between the eyebrows, and having emerged, takes the nectar of the five wisdoms from the tathāgatas who dwell in the world systems of the ten directions. It enters the golden door at the crown of the head called Jālandhara, goes into the opening at the junction of the teeth, rests in the wheel of enjoyment, brings joy to all the burned tathāgatas, and comes to abide in the maṇḍala of the navel. Thereby one does not see even so much as a fraction of the tip of a hair split a hundred thousand times going out or coming in, and one abides in joy, just as one pleases, at ease.

Through the yoga of one's own special deity
One should examine everything in this way.
It is thinking within non-thinking;
Mind too cannot be pointed out.
Through meditation that is beyond thought,
Existent and nonexistent are abandoned.
That mind which the mind thinks,
Whatever is found, should be grasped.
The khaṭvāṅga is the body of the deity;
The sound of the ḍamaru is wisdom.
By day the Blessed One is the hero;
By night he is taught to be the yoginī.
The emanation of the pure Dharma
Is shown in the form of Vārāhī.
śrī is nondual wisdom;
he is the abandonment of the collection of causes;
ru is freedom from arrangement;
ka is not abiding anywhere.

The yogin's mind, having come to rest,
Is what is taught as nirvāṇa.
At the time of the yogin's transference,
Heruka and the other glorious yogins,
Holding various flowers in their hands,
Bearing various victory banners and pennants,
Sounding the music of various instruments,
Make offerings with various songs.
This conceptual thought that is called "death"
Is carried off to the realm of the sky-goers.

From the Highest of the High, the Abhidhānottara Tantra, this is the fourteenth chapter, on the state of nondual union.

Next, something further is to be explained:
The divisions of meditation on the mothers (mātṛkās).
The union of the abodes of all the secret mantras,
The abode of all the tathāgatas,
All-knowing and all-accomplishing,
Pervading everything with great bliss,
The protector, the lord of all the buddhas,
Dwells in the hearts of all sentient beings.
aṃ āṃ iṃ īṃ uṃ ūṃ eṃ aiṃ oṃ auṃ aṃ āḥ hūṃ
This belongs to the Blessed One himself,
Heruka, the supreme lord.
In the sky, at the center of the hub
Of a stainless moon, one should meditate on him.
ṛṃ ṝṃ ḷṃ ḹṃ
The Ḍākinī dwells upon the lotus petals.
kaṃ khaṃ gaṃ ghaṃ ṅaṃ caṃ chaṃ jaṃ jhaṃ ñaṃ ṭaṃ ṭhaṃ ḍaṃ ḍhaṃ ṇaṃ taṃ thaṃ daṃ dhaṃ naṃ paṃ phaṃ baṃ bhaṃ maṃ
These are of Khaṇḍakapāla and the others,
And of Caṇḍākṣī and all the others:
They are the wheels of body, speech, and mind,
And they bestow every accomplishment.
yaṃ raṃ laṃ vaṃ śaṃ ṣaṃ saṃ haṃ
These are of Kākāsyā and the others,
And of Yamadāhī and all the others.
The syllable kṣaḥ, whose very nature
Is the form of the vital essence (śukra) of great bliss,
All-knowing and all-seeing,
Is the abode of all the buddhas,
All-pervading and ever empty,
Inexpressible, in the subtle center.
This is the unceasing, permanent mind;
Its own form is like space.
This is what binds oneself,
And this is what is skilled in liberating.
This dwells at the center of the navel,
And as the form of vital essence it abides as great bliss.
Through the union of body, speech, and mind,
It is to be placed at the center of the three wheels.
This is the supreme secret;
It is not to be taught to anyone.
The arms and hand emblems explained before,
And the armor as well, are likewise;
Having arranged them as explained before,
The aggregates, elements, and sense sources
Are the very purity of the hero's limbs,
And so too are the factors of awakening.
In forward and in reverse order,
One should arrange the wheels explained before.
All are white in color,
And the three wheels are sixteen.
The heroes too are to be arranged each in turn,
And the yoginīs each in turn as well;
Through union with their own method,
They abide in nonduality.
With the heroes' own wisdom consorts
They abide in nonduality.
The Ḍākinī and the others, and Śvānāsyā and the others,
Abide nondual with their own method.
That is the vajra essence.
This sacred teaching of the yoginīs,
Supremely secret and concealed,
Was requested by Vajragarbha
And spoken by the Blessed One.

From the Highest of the High, the Abhidhānottara Tantra, this is the fifteenth chapter, on generation through meditation on Śrī Heruka, the Ḍākinī, the heroes, and the yoginīs.

Next, something further is to be explained:
The meditation for beginners,
Supreme among the practices of yoga,
The instantaneous generation,
As taught by Vajragarbha.
He has two arms and one face,
Three eyes and a wrathful countenance;
With his outstretched right leg he presses down
On the heads of Bhairava and Kālarātri.
He is adorned with the six ornaments, a crossed vajra,
And a half moon;
He wears a tiger skin as his garment,
And is adorned with a garland of heads.
He holds a skull cup and khaṭvāṅga,
And brandishes the supreme vajra as well.
Vārāhī too embraces him,
Abiding with his color and with arms like his.
With her two thighs she clasps him evenly;
With skull cup, vajra, and threatening gesture
She menaces the directions, her hair hanging loose,
Wearing a girdle adorned with bone ornaments.
The retinue and the others of the maṇḍala
Have two arms and one face,
And are equally joined with the five mudrās;
Three-eyed and of fierce form,
All are seated on seats of pretas.
This is the meditation of the supreme meditation.
In the center the vajra Blood Drinker
Is blue in color, the great bliss.
Meditated upon at the center of the lotus,
The Ḍākinī is yellow in color.
The distinction between the heroes of the mind wheel
And the ḍākinīs is this:
The heroes are black in color,
And the ḍākinīs are white.
The heroes of the speech wheel
Are red in color and blaze intensely;
The ḍākinīs likewise are black:
One meditates on them in the two colors.
The heroes of the body wheel
Are beautifully adorned in moonlike pale yellow,
And the ḍākinīs with them are red;
They also take whatever form they wish.
Kākāsyā and the others are all green;
Yamadāhī and the others are all smoke-colored.
In this way the distinctions of color
Are to be known through supreme meditation.
The ḍākinīs hold skull cups and khaṭvāṅgas,
And are poised in dance with ḍamaru drums.
All of them have hair hanging loose,
Are clothed only in the directions, with laughing faces.
The heroes of the mind wheel
Embrace their consorts well, holding vajra and bell;
The yoginīs hold a skull cup and vajra,
Together with the threatening gesture.
The heroes of the speech wheel
Are adorned with lotus and bell;
The yoginīs hold a skull cup and lotus,
Together with the threatening gesture.
The heroes of the body wheel
Have a wheel and bell that blaze brightly;
The yoginīs have a wheel and the threatening gesture,
And a skull cup filled with marrow.

The goddesses Kākāsyā and the others
Hold a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga,
Together with a curved knife and the threatening gesture.
The goddesses Yamadāhī and the others
Have a ḍamaru drum, a head, and the threatening gesture,
And wear crowns made of garlands of skulls;
In the half-crossed posture they dance.
The hero stands with his right leg outstretched,
Entwined by the thighs of the ḍākinī,
Equally joined in nonduality,
The great bliss of compassion's passion.
Through being equally joined with the wheel of wisdom,
The bliss of bliss becomes one;
It arises perfectly from the taste of the Dharma.
Together with the seed syllable of one's own name,
One joins it evenly with the garland mantra.
Having joined the consonants evenly,
One joins it properly with two hūṃ syllables and phaṭ.
Thus the secret mantras of the heroes are properly joined.
For the yoginīs it is explained likewise:
In the center the seed syllable of their own name,
Evenly joined with the consonants,
And huṃ hūṃ phaṭ likewise:
The mantra practitioner should join them in this way.
In the center is the vajra Blood Drinker;
The wise one should recite this secret mantra:
oṃ śrī vajra he he ru ru kaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ hrīḥ h ha huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
These are the heart mantra and the near-heart mantra.
oṃ vajra vārāhi huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajra vairocanīye huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
These are the heart mantra and the near-heart mantra of the goddess.
At the four times, according to the rite,
The yogin should perform the recitation.
In front of him, from the pores of his body hair,
He emanates the Dharma and enjoyments.
In order to benefit sentient beings,
He should display the wheel of Dharma.
Having liberated the six classes of beings,
He places them in the unsurpassed state.
The mantra practitioner applies this as he wishes.
And in the center of the five families,
The one who pervades the center of the three families,
The lord of the three realms,
In whom all the buddhas are perfectly gathered,
Is the division of body, speech, and mind.

From the Abhidhānottara Tantra, this is the sixteenth chapter, teaching the meditation practice for beginners.

As fully explained by Vajragarbha, This brings about every accomplishment: Through the rite of the four-armed one, Those of middling faculties should meditate. Through the practice of instantaneous generation, They should cultivate the meditations. He has four arms and four faces, Three eyes, fierce and terrifying; He stands in the posture with his right leg outstretched, Trampling Bhairava and Kālarātri. His body bears a necklace of human heads; He has a supreme crown of skulls, Beautified by a half moon, And marked with a crossed vajra. With the six mudrās, in fierce form, In colors white and terrifying, Blue, green, and red, One should visualize his faces above. He holds a skull cup and khaṭvāṅga, A vajra and bell, and a ḍamaru drum, And is equally joined with Vajravārāhī, Pressing the breasts of the goddess. He wears a tiger skin as his garment. She wears the supreme garment of the directions, Her hair hanging loose, with a girdle of bone ornaments, Marked by his arms, his faces, and his color. The Ḍākinī is blue and yellow; Lāmā is green and yellow; Khaṇḍarohā is red and green; Rūpiṇī is yellow and red. They hold a skull cup and khaṭvāṅga, And, supreme, hold a curved knife and a head; They sound the many sounds of the ḍamaru drum. Naked, their hair hanging loose, They wear diadems made of skulls. Three-eyed, with fierce faces, Their own colors and their arms likewise, They dance in the half-crossed posture, Trampling the four māras beneath their feet, Gazing with desire and imagining. The heroes of the mind wheel One should visualize as blue and yellow. Likewise the vajra ḍākinīs One should visualize as white and blue. Holding skull cup and khaṭvāṅga, They embrace well with vajra and bell. The various sounds of the ḍamaru drum, The vajra sound, are made complete. Skull cup and threatening gesture, Curved knife and human skull they hold: The hands of the goddesses are likewise, And with their two thighs they entwine well. He stands in the posture with his right leg outstretched, Contorted like the Great Terrifier, Wearing a diadem of a vajra garland: The heroes have the vajra as their mark. The heroes of the speech wheel Are red and black and terrifying, Pervaded by the lotus heroes, Holding their own hand emblems; The lotus ḍākinīs likewise Have bodies green and white. The heroes of the body wheel, too, Have bodies yellow and green; The heroes are wholly pervaded by wheels, And they also bestow the rule of a universal monarch. The wheel ḍākinīs likewise Are white and red and captivating, Perfectly endowed with their own emblems, Turning the wheel of Dharma. The four ḍākinīs, Kākāsyā and the others, Are of various colors and captivating. They hold a skull cup and khaṭvāṅga, A curved knife, a head, and a ḍamaru drum. The goddesses Yamadāhī and the others Are likewise smoke-colored and red. Yama's staff and curved knife, Skull cup and khaṭvāṅga they hold. The ḍākinīs, the mothers of the retinue and the rest, Are seated upon seats of pretas. Entwined equally with their two thighs, The ḍākinīs are in great delight. Their hair hanging loose, wearing the garment of the directions, They wear crowns made of garlands of skulls. All of them hold the five mudrās, The retinue of heroes and ḍākinīs. Three-eyed, fierce, and contorted, The heroes revel in the food of desire, And gaze with the names of passion. Following the sequence of the rite explained before, The secret mantra with the seed syllable of one's own name: The heroes' secret mantra is explained here, And the ḍākinīs' likewise. This very root secret mantra, And the supreme secret mantra likewise: oṃ śrī vajra he he ru ru kaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā oṃ hrīḥ h ha huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajra vārāhi baṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā oṃ vajra vairocanīye huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā When, having meditated on them all, one sees them, The lord and the retinue and the rest, Having seen that wheel of wisdom, One should properly confer empowerment and cause it to enter. Having made them one through meditation, One should satisfy them with supreme nectar. Through the practice of wisdom recitation, Having emanated the supreme host of wrathful ones, When one sees the heroes and ḍākinīs, Through the sequence of body, speech, and mind, The one who holds the samaya should perform the recitation. The retinue and the rest, the heroes and the ḍākinīs: This is the secret mantra of them all, And it is explained as the ultimate recitation.

From the Highest of the High, the Abhidhānottara Tantra, this is the seventeenth chapter, teaching the meditation for those of middling faculties.

Next, something further is to be explained,
The secret of Vajragarbha:
Whatever stages of reality there are,
In order to benefit the practitioner,
The meditation for those of sharp faculties is explained.
The practices of the generation stage
Are to be cultivated through that meditation.
The wisdom of omniscience
And the wisdom that knows all aspects of the path:
Joining the vowels and consonants evenly,
One should make oneself into Heruka.
The protector has three faces and six arms,
Three eyes, and a contorted face.
He stands in the posture with his right leg outstretched,
Pressing with his feet upon the heads
Of Bhairava and Kālarātri.
He wears a human skin as his garment,
Is adorned with a half moon,
Has a crossed vajra set upon his head,
And wears a crown made of a garland of skulls;
His body is sealed with the six mudrās.
He is equally joined with Vajravārāhī,
Who, in nondual union,
Entwines him well with her two thighs,
Naked, her hair hanging loose,
Wearing a girdle of bone ornaments
And a garland of skulls,
Intoxicated with the bliss of compassion.
Equally joined with vajra and bell,
He delights in bliss with the passion of desire.
He holds a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga,
Bears a sword and a ḍamaru drum,
And in another hand the head of Brahmā,
And is adorned with the six mudrās.
She is marked by his body color, his faces, and his arms,
Except that she holds a curved knife.
Sky-colored, pale yellow, red, and white:
The three doors of liberation and the three bodies.
All the retinue and the rest, the ḍākinīs,
The heroes, the yoginīs, and the ḍākinīs,
All have three faces and six arms
And three eyes, and stand in the posture with the right leg outstretched,
Firmly established upon seats of pretas,
Abiding in nondual union.
The Ḍākinī is white, blue, and red;
Lāmā is green, blue, and white;
Khaṇḍarohā is red, white, and blue;
Rūpiṇī is yellow, blue, and red.

These are three-eyed, naked, with their hair hanging loose, standing with the right leg outstretched upon pretas, and they hold a skull cup and khaṭvāṅga, a sword, a wheel, a curved knife, a head, and a ḍamaru drum.

As for the heroes and yoginīs of the mind wheel: in the east are Khaṇḍakapāla and Pracaṇḍā, blue, white, and red. In the north are Mahākaṅkāla and Caṇḍākṣī, red, white, and blue. In the west are Kaṅkāla and Prabhāvatī, white, red, and green. In the south are Vikaṭadaṃṣṭra and Mahānāsā, green, white, and red. In the southeast are Suravairin and Vīramatī, red, blue, and white. In the southwest are Amitābha and Kharvarī, yellow, blue, and white. In the northwest are Vajraprabha and Laṅkeśvarī, blue, white, and red. In the northeast are Vajradeha and Drumacchāyā, green, white, and red.

In the east of the speech wheel are Aṅkurika and Airāvatī, red, blue, and white. In the north are Vajrajaṭila and Mahābhairavā, blue, white, and red. In the west are Mahāvīra and Vāyuvegā, green, white, and red. In the south are Vajrahūṃkāra and Surābhakṣī, yellow, blue, and red. In the southeast are Subhadra and Śyāmādevī, green, yellow, and red. In the southwest are Vajrabhadra and Subhadrā, blue, white, and red. In the northwest are Mahābhairava and Hayakarṇā, red, blue, and yellow. In the northeast are Virūpākṣa and Khagānanā, white, blue, and green.

In the east of the body wheel are Mahābala and Cakravegā, white, blue, and yellow. In the north are Vajraratna and Khaṇḍarohā, yellow, blue, and green. In the west are Hayagrīva and Śauṇḍinī, red, blue, and white. In the south are Ākāśagarbha and Cakravarmiṇī, blue, red, and green. In the southeast are the Blood Drinker and Suvīrā, green, yellow, and red. In the southwest are Padmanarteśvara and Mahābalā, white, blue, and green. In the northwest are Vairocana and Cakravartinī, green, red, and yellow. In the northeast are Vajrasattva and Mahāvīryā, sky-colored light blue, yellow, and green.

At the eastern gate is Kākāsyā, blue, yellow, and red. In the north is Ulūkāsyā, green, red, and white. In the west is Śvānāsyā, yellow, blue, and green. In the south is Śūkarāsyā, red, blue, and green. In the southeast is Yamadāhī, white, blue, and green. In the southwest is Yamadūtī, blue, white, and red. In the northwest is Yamadaṃṣṭrī, smoke-colored, red, and white. In the northeast is Yamamathanī, red, blue, and white.

Khaṇḍakapāla and the others, and Pracaṇḍā and the others, are equally joined with vajra and bell, and hold a sword, a ḍamaru drum, a head, a skull cup, and a khaṭvāṅga. Lotus and lotus bell, Wheel and wheel bell: The two, of body and of speech, Are just as with the mind wheel. The goddesses have the ḍamaru drum and sword, And the curved knife likewise. Kākāsyā and the others hold a sword, a ḍamaru drum, a curved knife, a skull cup, a khaṭvāṅga, a head, and a bell. Yamadāhī and the others hold a skull cup, a khaṭvāṅga, a sword, a hook, a ḍamaru drum, a bell, and a head.

All of them, moreover, are three-eyed, adorned with the five mudrās, and wear human skins. The heroes stand with the right leg outstretched; they wear garlands of skulls and the diadems of their own families, stand upon pretas, and are entwined well by the two thighs of the yoginīs, who are naked, their hair hanging loose, and wear girdles of bone ornaments. The goddesses Kākāsyā and the others and Yamadāhī and the others are naked, their hair hanging loose; they stand upon pretas with the right leg outstretched, are three-eyed, adorned with the five mudrās, and wear garlands of skulls. Their bellies protrude, their sexual organs are long, and their faces bare their fangs; they are intoxicated with the taste of compassion's passion, and dreadful with their terrifying movements. Having meditated on all of this in this way, without omission, When one sees the wheel of wisdom, One should make offerings with wisdom water and the rest, with arghya, And with the supreme samaya substances. As soon as one sees it, one confers empowerment as well. Having made offerings and caused it to dissolve, According to the rite of wisdom recitation, Having set them in place, one should fully perform the scattering and the drinking. Through the practice of the life breath and its control, One obtains the accomplishments one desires. Having recited for the samaya wheel, Together with the five deathless wisdoms, One recites the secret mantra with wrathful words; It bestows the accomplishment of bliss. One should perform the entire maṇḍala, without omission, And, resting in equipoise, perform the recitation. According to the rite of wisdom and method, The recitation of yoga bestows the great aim. The yoginīs, the ḍākinīs, and the heroes Bestow even the supreme accomplishment directly. oṃ śrī vajra a he he ru ru kaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ a ā i ī u ū e ai o au aṃ aḥ hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā oṃ hrīḥ h ha huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajra vairocanīye huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā oṃ sarva buddha ḍākinīye vajra varṇaṇnīye huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā These are the heart mantras and near-heart mantras of the glorious Blood Drinker and of Vārāhī. oṃ ḍākinī ḍāṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ lāme lāṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ khaṇḍaroheṇīye rūṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ kara kara kaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ pracaṇḍe huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ kuru kuru maṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ caṇḍākṣi huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ bhandha bhandha kaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ prabhavati huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ trāsaya trāsaya vaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ mahānāse huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ kṣobhaya kṣobhaya suṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vīramati huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ hrauṃ hrauṃ āṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ kharvari huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ hraḥ hraḥ vaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ laṅkaiśvari huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ pheṃ pheṃ vaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ drumacchāye huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ phaṭ phaṭ aṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ airāvati huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ daha daha vaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ mahābhairava huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ paca paca maṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vāyuvege huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ bhakṣa bhakṣa vaṃ vasa rudhira antra mālā valaṃ vani huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ surabhakṣi huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ gṛhṇa gṛhṇa suṃ sapta pātāla gata bhujaṃga sarvaṃ vā tarjajya tarjaj huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ śyāmadevi huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ ākaḍḍhya ākaḍḍhya vaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ subhadre huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ hriṃ hriṃ maṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ hayakarṇeṇ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ jṇauṃ jṇauṃ vaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ khagāna huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ kṣmāṃ kṣmāṃ saṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ cakravege huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ hāṃ hāṃ traṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ khaṇḍarohe huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ hīṃ hīṃ haṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ śauṇḍini huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ hūṃ hūṃ haṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ cakravarmiṇi huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ kili kili heṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ suvīre huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ sili sili vaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ mahābale huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ hili hili vaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ cakravartini huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ dhili dhili vaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ mahāvīrye huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ kākāsye huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ ulūkāsye huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ śvānāsye huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ sūkarāsye huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ yamadāḍhi huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ yamadūti huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ yamadaṃṣṭi huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ yamamathani huṃ hūṃ phaṭ Having recited these and meditated In their proper order, And having meditated on the nectar of wisdom, One abides in union with one's own deity.

From the Highest of the High, the Abhidhānottara Tantra, this is the eighteenth chapter, the further teaching on the meditation for those of sharp faculties.

Vajragarbha said:
Next the most sublime of the supreme is explained:
The vows of equal union,
Equal union with all the buddhas,
The supreme vow of the ḍākinī.
Having seen the instantaneous entry,
One meditates on Vajrasattva and emanates.
Arising from the vowels and consonants,
One makes oneself into Heruka.
His faces are yellow, blue, and green,
And the one above is white and peaceful.
His crown ornament is a diadem of skulls;
Three-eyed, compassionate and fierce,
He is adorned by Vārāhī.
Upon the heads of Bhairava and Kālarātri
He stands with his right leg outstretched.
Fierce, he tames those who are hard to tame,
And subdues all obstructors.
He wears a half moon and matted locks,
And bears the imprint of a crossed vajra.
His body is marked with the six mudrās;
These mudrās are the supreme vow.
He wears a tiger skin as his garment,
And is adorned with a garland of heads.
Vajravārāhī is red,
Naked, her hair hanging loose.
With his body, his face, and his emblems,
She entwines him well with her two thighs.
She wears a crown of a garland of skulls;
Lip is pressed tightly to lip;
She wears a girdle of little bells,
Intoxicated with the joy of the family.
Equally joined with vajra and bell,
He holds the breasts of Vārāhī.
He holds a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga,
And holds the supreme noose and hook as well.
Having stretched out the skin of Brahmā,
He holds it with his hands above his head.
The melodious sound of the ḍamaru drum
Fills everything with the sound of the Dharma.
On the left and on the right
One should visualize the four ḍākinīs:
On the right side the Ḍākinī and Lāmā,
In the north Khaṇḍarohā and Rūpiṇī.
Blue and saffron, white and yellow,
Three-eyed, contorted and fierce,
White, blue, green, red, and saffron,
They blaze with intense brilliance.

When they are meditated upon on the right side,
They bestow every accomplishment.
On the left side, red, blue, and green,
And likewise white for the upper face;
Green, red, and yellow,
And blue above: that too is bliss.
She stands with her right leg outstretched,
Wearing the supreme garment of the directions,
Her hair hanging loose, terrifying,
Adorned with a garland of heads,
Trampling the four māras beneath her feet,
Adorned with the five mudrās.
She holds a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga,
And holds a vajra spear in her hand;
She wears a human skin as her garment;
A bell and a ḍamaru drum likewise,
And she holds a head and a curved knife in her hands.
Her girdle of little bells resounds,
Her anklets jingle, ru lu ru lu.
Upon a variegated lotus,
At the center of a sun disk,
They are seated together on a single seat.
In the heart are the samaya being and the wisdom being,
With that color, those arms and hand emblems:
Vajrasattva Heruka,
With a diadem of Akṣobhya.
Having meditated on all of them in this way,
Together with the major and minor marks,
And having made them one with wisdom,
One recites the vow of the ḍākinīs:
oṃ āḥ śrī vajra he he ru ru kaṃ ḍākinī jāla saṃvaraṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ hrīḥ h ha hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ āḥ vajra vārāhi hye vaṃ ḍākinī jāla sambaraṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ vajra vairocanīye huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
These are the heart mantras and near-heart mantras of the glorious Blood Drinker and of Vārāhī.
oṃ vajra ḍākini huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ vajra lāme huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ vajra khaṇḍarohe huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
oṃ vajra rūpiṇi huṃ hūṃ phaṭ
With these, in their proper order,
One should perform recitation and meditation.
In the sequence that follows the four times,
With samaya and vow inseparable,
One should also eat the five lamps.
One should always meditate within a charnel ground.
Through practice for seven nights,
The accomplishment of invisibility is certainly attained,
And one goes everywhere and can also make oneself seen.
Taking various forms, in great bliss,
One should eat the foods,
And likewise drink the drinks for thirst.
Whoever is clad only in the directions
Remains intoxicated, and as if intoxicated.
In a place where there is not even a shadow,
The great yogin practices.
Together with mahārudhira, great blood,
And equally joined with excrement and water,
Many various kinds of māṃsa, flesh:
One eats this food with single-pointed mind.
Endowed with union with the inner self,
One should always proceed with devotion.
The self of union, even when not in meditative equipoise,
Is seen with the eye of wisdom.

From the Highest of the High, the Abhidhānottara Tantra, this is the nineteenth chapter, the rite of the vow of union with the four ḍākinīs.

Next, something further is to be explained.
In accordance with the excellent rite,
He has eight faces and sixteen arms;
Or else twelve faces
And twenty-four arms;
Or else the faces of various animals,
And one should give him a hundred arms.
Or else the family of the tathāgatas,
Bodhisattvas and heroes,
Ḍākinīs and dhāraṇī goddesses,
And the perfections likewise,
With faces distinct from one another,
Various distinctions of color,
And arms from a thousand up to a hundred thousand,
Holding various hand emblems,
Having faces of various kinds,
And performing dances of various kinds,
He stands swaying, with his right leg outstretched.

He is seen pressing face down beneath his feet Mahābhairava, Kālarātri, and the others, Brahmā, Indra, Upendra, Rudra, Viṣṇu, the Youth Who Scatters All, Kārtika, Mahākāla, Nandikeśvara, Yama, Varuṇa, Kubera, the Moon, the Sun, the various māras, and all the obstructors. He is dreadful, his countenance transformed, fierce, with bared fangs, laughing, repulsive, utterly terrifying, heroic, graceful, fierce, with quivering brows, haughty, peaceful, moving, with darting eyes, mouth gaping wide, playful, tongue lolling, and he bestows the supreme through the various aesthetic moods.

With a garland of skulls and a half moon,
And a crossed vajra pressed upon his head,
He has a diadem of the five buddhas,
And Vajrasattva adorns the crown of his head.
Three-eyed, he wears a tiger skin;
His body is adorned with a garland of fresh heads;
The six mudrās are complete upon his body,
And he is decked with ornaments of garlands.
Vārāhī entwines his body,
Clad only in the directions, her hair hanging loose,
Two-armed and one-faced,
Wearing a girdle of bone ornaments,
And adorned with a garland of skulls.
She embraces him with vajra, threatening gesture, and skull cup;
With her two thighs she embraces him evenly,
Pervaded by joy and intoxicated.
He is equally joined with vajra and bell,
Pressing the breasts of the goddess,
And holds a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga,
And an outstretched elephant hide,
A hook, a noose, and an axe,
A trident, and a head,
A ḍamaru drum and a curved knife,
An arrow and a bow likewise,
A skull staff and a short spear,
The threatening gesture and a pestle,
A wheel, a sword, a jewel,
A lotus, a crossed vajra,
A staff with a vajra handle,
A club, a spear, and a paṭṭiśa,
Yama's staff and a dagger,
A conch, a horn, and a paṭaha drum,
A lute, a flute, a great clay drum,
And a clay drum: he holds weapons as his hand emblems.
Seen with their sharp points,
Various emblems appear in various ways,
For as many arms as there are, up to a hundred thousand.
So too with the various mudrās,
And so too with the various rites.
The retinue, the Ḍākinī and the others,
And all of the heroes,
And the yoginīs of various kinds,
Of infinite colors, of various colors,
Hold various hand emblems.
Wearing the garment of the directions, their hair hanging loose,
Possessing the bliss of the various moods,
Dreadful, fierce, laughing "ha ha,"
They revel in various dances,
And in songs, music, and moods likewise.
Various samaya foods,
Various lovely flowers,
Perfumes and incense, and garments likewise,
Lovely garlands of lamps:
These are perfect offerings,
Of various colors, supreme and sublime.
Having meditated in this way, the mantra practitioner,
Having completed it with supreme yoga,
Then recites the garland mantra as well,
Having blended in the twenty-two syllables.
The goddess Vajravārāhī
Should likewise be meditated upon in this way:
Beautiful with various faces,
Holding various hand emblems,
Clad only in the directions, her hair hanging loose,
She is the perfection of awakening,
And likewise all those who hold the grounds.

Having performed the rite in this way, One should recite the secret mantra with a rosary: oṃ śrī vajra he he ru ru kaṃ kara kara kuru kuru bandha bandha trāsaya trāsaya kṣobhaya kṣobhaya hrauṃ hrauṃ hraḥ hraḥ pheṃ pheṃ phaṭ phaṭ daha daha paca paca bhakṣa bhakṣa vasarudhira antramālāvalaṃvini gṛhṇa gṛhṇa saptapātālagatabhujaṃg sarvamapā tarjjaya tarjajya ākaḍhya ākaḍhya hrīṃ hrīṃ jṇauṃ jṇauṃ kṣmāṃ kṣmāṃ hāṃ hāṃ hīṃ hīṃ hūṃ hūṃ kili kili sili sili hili hili dhili dhili hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ phaṭ ḍākinījālasambaraṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā This is the supreme heart mantra, Virtuous, accomplishing all actions. If the hero, with the seed syllable and his own name, And the yoginī, with devotion, constantly recite it, Everything without exception is accomplished, And one travels to the abodes of beings. At that very moment, immediately, The supreme vow is accomplished. This secret mantra of Vajravārāhī Bestows every bliss that one desires: oṃ vajravārāhi protaṅge protaṅge hana hana praṇāna kiṅkiṇi khiṅkhiṇi dhuna dhuna vajrahaste śoṣaya śoṣaya vajrakapāla khaṭvādgadhāriṇi mahāpiśate mānasāśani mānuṣa antraprāvṛtte sānnidhya naraśiromālāgranthitadhāriṇi sumbhani sumbhe hana hana prāṇān sarvapāpasatvānāṃ sarvapaśūnāṃ mahāmāṃsacchedanī krodhamūrte daṃṣṭākarālini mahāmudre śrīherukadevasya agramahiṣi sahasraśire sahasrabāhave śatasahasra ānane jvalitatejase jvalamukhe piṃgalalocane vajraśarīre vajra āsane mili mili timili timili he he ha ha hūṃ hūṃ kha kha dhu dhu ru ru dhuru dhuru muru muru advaite mahāyogini paṭhitasiddhe dreṃ dhaṃ dreṃ dhaṃ graṃ graṃ he he ha ha bhīme hasa hasa vīre hā hā ho ho hūṃ hūṃ trailokyavināśāni śatasahasrakoṭitathāgataparivārite hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ siṃharupe khaḥ gjarūpe gaḥ trailokyodare samudramekhale grasa grasa hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ vīrādvaite huṃ hūṃ hāḥ hā mahāpaśūmohani mahāyogīśvari tvaṃ ḍākinī sarvalokānāṃ bandhanī sadyaḥ pratyayakīraṇi huṃ hūṃ phaṭ bhūtatrāsani mahāvīre paramasiddhe yogīśvari phaṭ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā This is the root mantra of Vajravārāhī. This is the supreme Vajravārāhī, Accomplished as the supreme heart. Whoever recites it and meditates, By that one all actions are accomplished; Without doubt they are accomplished in accordance with the sūtra. The worship is of oneself, in one's own place, And never anything external. Of that same accomplished Blood Drinker, The secret mantra endowed with eight roots: oṃ namo bhagavate vīreśāya huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ mahākalpa agni sannibhāya huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ jaṭamukuṭodakaṭāya huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ daṃṣṭākarālograbhīṣanamukhāya huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ sahasrabhujabhāsurāya huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ paraśupaśodyataśūlakhaṭvāṃgadhāriṇe huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vyāghrajina aṃbaradharāya huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ mahādhūmrāndhakāravapuṣāya huṃ hūṃ phaṭ This is the secret mantra of the Blood Drinker; One should meditate upon oneself and make worship. oṃ namo bhagavati vajravārāhi vaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ ārya aparājite trailogyamāte mahāvidyeśvari huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ sarvabhūtabhayāvahe mahāvajre huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajrāsane ajite aparājite vaśyaṃkari netrabhrāmaṇi huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ śoṣaṇi roṣaṇi krodhani karālini huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ trāsani māraṇi suprabhedani parājaye huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vijaye jaṃbhani staṃbhani mohani huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajravārāhi mahāyogini kāmeśvari khage huṃ hūṃ phaṭ namaḥ is to be applied to all of them. With this secret mantra one should perform all actions. Having meditated upon oneself As Vajravārāhī of the vowels and consonants, Endowed with the mantras of the eight roots, One should also worship oneself. Having blessed the four faces, One worships with oṃ sumbhani sumbhas. Or else, with all four faces as vajra, And at her navel the glorious Heruka, Having meditated upon oneself, One should likewise meditate on the retinue as well. Eating beef and the like, one accomplishes the practice. Those malicious toward the teaching of the Sugata and toward beings, Such creatures are declared to be the ones: Those who steal the property of the Three Jewels, Those who commit the deeds of immediate retribution, Whoever reviles the master, And those who revile the Great Vehicle. Having slain those beasts, One should eat them together with the yoginīs. The summoning of those beasts, too, Is to be done by the practitioner through prior generation. The seven beasts, and likewise the beasts, Are the donkey, the human, and the tortoise, The dog, the camel, the jackal, and the horse. These lamps of the beasts Are known as the limbs of the divisions of accomplishment. The beasts are distinguished by caste and color. Among the four-footed, the elephant; Among birds, the goose is substituted; Among the animals, one whose lips move. They are offered as food into the mouths of the deities. This is taught as the examination of the beasts. The practice for accomplishment is explained: Crow, hawk, and jackal, The twice-born, and one of great mantra, Crane, goose, and the kaṅbuka bird: With these excellent ones, perform the fire offering and the torma. One who desires accomplishments should meditate. With the milk of a black bitch, Together with black beans and rice, One should perform the fire offering in the mouth of a corpse. One should perform a hundred and eight fire offerings. Then that corpse rises up And will say, "What shall I do?" Going beneath the earth, or the sword, Subjugation, or bestowing benefit, Or pills, eye ointment, or gold, Or swift-footedness, or the taking of the essence: Having granted any one of these, It departs, and not otherwise. Messengers above and below are accomplished. In this tantra there is no doubt. From the Highest of the Highest, the Abhidhānottara Tantra: the twentieth chapter, on the rite of the vow of yoga.

Then, further, I shall explain The sequence of protection and averting. The supreme protection, hidden in other tantras And hard to find, I shall fully explain in this tantra, The Abhidhāna Tantra, highest of the highest. One should have no doubt about it. In order to protect sentient beings, One should draw an eight-petaled lotus. At the center of the lotus, with its anthers, And behind that a crossed vajra, One should correctly apply the secret mantra of Sumbha. Outside it one should make a girdle, And draw sixteen petals. There the divisions are to be arranged, And with that the outside is encircled. What the wise one draws at the center Is a vajra with twelve prongs. What is to be placed at its center Is hūṃ, fearsome and terrifying. At the center of the hūṃ is the letter vaṃ; At the center of the vaṃ, three letters, To be drawn interspersed with the name of the one to be accomplished. The four ḍākinīs Are to be placed circling from its left: ḍāṃ laṃ khaṃ rūṃ are placed there, Together with ṛ ṝ ḷ ḹ. Placing the ḍākinīs, one should draw them Thoroughly interspersed with the name. Following the order of the wheel of mind, The wise one should place on the petals khaṃ praṃ on the eastern petal; In the north it is kaṃ caṃ; In the west, only kaṃ paṃ; In the south it is vaṃ maṃ. In the directions from Agni's to Īśāna's: suṃ vaṃ iṃ khaṃ vaṃ laṃ hraṃ. By these, in their proper order, Interspersed with the letter hūṃ, The seed syllables should be drawn. kaṃ khaṃ gaṃ ghaṃ ṅaṃ caṃ chaṃ jaṃ: these are drawn on the girdle of the lotus. The vajra with twelve divisions Encircles it, adorned with drops. Outside the sixteen petals Draw a wheel with sixteen spokes. Here, beginning with the deity of the east, They are to be placed circling from the left: The ḍākinīs and the heroes. āṃ ā vaṃ maṃ vaṃ maṃ paṃ suṃ suṃ śāṃ vaṃ suṃ muṃ haṃ viṃ khaṃ maṃ caṃ raṃ khaṃ haṃ saṃ aṃ ciṃ haṃ suṃ paṃ maṃ vaiṃ caṃ vaṃ maṃ These, interspersed with hri, And interspersed with oṃ, should be drawn. The name of the one to be accomplished is placed inside; One draws according to whatever the rite may be. jhaṃ ñaṃ ṭaṃ ṭhaṃ ḍaṃ ḍhaṃ ṇaṃ taṃ thaṃ daṃ dhaṃ naṃ paṃ phaṃ baṃ bhaṃ maṃ: the girdle is drawn on the outside. Outside the wheel, within a square, Draw the doors, joined together, As follows: From Kākāsyā and the rest in their order, Place the seed syllables of Yamamathanī and the rest: kaṃ aṃ svāṃ sūṃ yaṃ yaṃ yaṃ yaṃ yaṃ raṃ laṃ vaṃ śaṃ ṣaṃ saṃ haṃ, Joining them, and interspersing with hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ, And also interspersing them with the name. Above the doors One should draw four-pronged half vajras. At the corners One should draw sun and moon and half vajras. Above that, one looks toward the faces Of the eight wrathful ones, expressed in eight lines. In the middle of the outer girdle One should draw these averting mantras: namaḥ smantakāyavāgacittavajraherukānāṃ oṃ hūṃ vajraherukacakravarti sarvalokikalokodtarāṇi yantramantratantracūrṇaṇprayogādīni yena kenacid kritāni sarvāṇi ākaḍḍhya ākaḍḍhya tarjjaya tarjjaya gṛhṇa gṛhṇa bhakṣaya bhakṣaya trāsaya trāsaya hana hana daha daha paca paca kṣmāṃ kṣmāṃ jnauṃ jṇauṃ matha matha vidhvansaya vidhvanya śatasahasradhacūrṇaṇya oṃ phaṭ vajraherukoṣṇīṣacakravarttitasyevamukhaṃ cakṣur bandha bandha hūṃ hūṃ amukasya śāntiṃ kuru hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ svāhā Having encircled it completely with this, Place it in the middle of the belly of kṣa. Outside the letter kṣa, Having encircled it completely with thā, Encircle it completely with the marks of the vowels. Having meditated on oneself as Heruka, One should also recite the seven-syllable mantra: oṃ hrīḥ h ha amukasya śāntiṃ kuru śāntiṃ kuru hūṃ hūṃ pāpaṃ phaṭ From a woman of low caste, or a brahmin woman, Or especially from a king's daughter, Having taken the self-arisen flower, Mixed with the elephant flower, And combined in equal measure with the awakening mind, Blended together with rocana, And likewise the water of lotus and vajra, Mixed with the great eye ointment, One should draw the wheel of the supreme deity, Encircled by a garland of vajras, On human skin, or on birch bark, Or else a preta's cloth will also do. Having taken butter and milk Endowed with the five nectars, One recites the secret mantra of eight lines. Likewise the one who keeps the observance makes worship. A vase of gold or of silver, Filled with the nectar water of the five wisdoms: Having placed the wheel in its middle, Wind the wheel with white thread. Recite the garland secret mantra over it. Then one should bestow the empowerment. It makes the sick free of disease, And it also makes the healthy sick. Having placed it in the middle Of intoxicating liquor, the wise one Makes the healthy sick, Following whatever the rite may be. For any action whatsoever, Encircle it with the garland mantra. If the wheel for accomplishing yoga Is concealed inside a head, The ḍākinīs will, without doubt, Bestow their blessing upon it. Everything one desires will be accomplished, And accomplishment will come without obstacles. From the Highest of the Highest, the Abhidhānottara Tantra: the twenty-first chapter, on protection and averting. Then her meditation is explained, Which benefits all sentient beings. In an instant, oneself Is the averting goddess, in sequence: Four-faced, with sixteen arms, Pressing beneath her lotus Obstructors of many vicious kinds, She sits with her right leg extended, Abiding in the center of a moon maṇḍala. White, blue, red, and yellow, Adorned with a face on top, Complete with every ornament, Adorned with white garments, Crowned with the five buddhas, Supreme in truly restoring the dying to life, Three-eyed, with gentle speech, But vicious and fierce toward others. With the threatening forefinger she brandishes the vajra, Supreme in holding the bell and the noose; She holds vajra, sword, and spear, Axe, skull cup, and hook, Bow and khaṭvāṅga, And draws the arrow taut. Hammer, wheel, and the gesture of fearlessness: She bestows every accomplishment. Or else she has eight faces, On her right and left sides. Those on the right are blue, red, and yellow, Fierce, laughing "ha ha." Those on the left are green, red, And the color of smoke. The one on top is beautiful and peaceful, Her body the color of the moon.

At her heart is the wheel, In the very center of a moon maṇḍala. With faith, deeply settled in meditative equipoise, One should recite the vidyā-mantra that restores the dying to life: oṃ amṛta sumukhe jīvaṃ praveśaya praveśaya sarvavaṃjrapadmasamayoge mahābodhicidtavisbāpitamūrte huṃ hūṃ a ā aṃ aḥ he svāhā By reciting this some ten thousand times, One restores all the dying to life. Untimely death is pacified, And one is certainly raised from death. Otherwise, when the yoginīs are pleased, They perform wonders, Dancing each on her own. With hi hi there is joy, With ho ho there is delight, With he he they speak words, With huṃ hūṃ there is completion. huṃ hūṃ is meditative concentration. With phaṭ phaṭ there is killing, With hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ, the ritual dagger. With jaḥ jaḥ they are well drawn in. sa sa makes things true; It is the sound that means "certainly excellent." hūṃ vaṃ is killing. hūṃ bruṃ hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ is grinding to ashes. With ha ha there is making clear. bhuṃ bhuṃ produces splendor. With hriṃ hriṃ they are well drawn in. tryaṃ tryaṃ is worship. With saṃ saṃ the fruit of all accomplishments is bestowed. hi hi is bewildering. laṃ laṃ is auspiciousness. di di is holding. With jiṃ jiṃ they are well drawn in. hūṃ hūṃ is saṃsāra. hri hri is killing. jaṃ jaṃ brings about stupefaction. With ho ho one is to be made enamored. haṃ haṃ is protecting. ho ho is delight. ṭaṃ ṭaṃ is the vicious. gūṃ gūṃ is flowers. gaṃ gaṃ is scent. raṃ raṃ is the secret. By these, in their proper order, One abides by this sequence Of the rites of pacifying and the rest. From the Highest of the Highest, the Abhidhānottara Tantra: the twenty-second chapter, teaching the meditation that performs all the actions of raising from death. Then another secret is to be explained: The reality of mantra, the sequence of the rite, Spoken by Vajragarbha And declared the supreme of all secrets. The supreme bliss of the yoginīs, The sequence of the lineage of the yoginīs, Is secret, secret, and supremely secret. Through the practice of the generation stage It is the supreme cultivation of meditative concentration. Having made oneself into Heruka, One is completely pervaded by the ḍākinīs. His body is red and greatly terrifying, Emerging from the five wisdoms. His faces are red, blue, green, And yellow, and the peaceful upper face is white. He has three eyes and twelve arms, And sits with his right leg extended. Great Bhairava and Kālarātri, Having come for refuge, are pressed beneath his feet. With the six mudrās and a half moon, He bears a crossed vajra on his locks. He wears an elephant hide as his garment. He holds vajra and bell together, Sword and spear, supreme in terror, Holds skull cup and khaṭvāṅga, Supreme in bearing noose and hook, The ḍamaru and the head of Brahmā, And is adorned with a garland of heads. He has a crown ornament of a garland of skulls, Adorned with the five buddhas. Before him is Vajravārāhī, Bearing his color and his hand emblems, Supreme in bearing noose and hook, The ḍamaru and the head of Brahmā, And adorned with a garland of heads. She twines closely about him with her two thighs, Joined equally in nondual union, Adorned with the five mudrās. Arisen from the wisdom of kṣaḥ, With its body, faces, and hand emblems, Abiding in the center of a moon maṇḍala, One should meditate on the wisdom being. Emerging from the twelve parts, The commitment being, the great bliss, The emergence of omniscience: Here one recites the mantra tantra. oṃ śrī vajra he he ru ru kaṃ a ā i ī u ū e ai o au aṃ aḥ ḍākinījālasambaraṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā oṃ a ā aṃ aḥ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ a ā aṃ aḥ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ These are the heart mantra and the near-heart mantra. oṃ vajravārāhi a ā i ī u ū e ai o au aṃ aḥ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā oṃ vajravairocanīye a ā aṃ aḥ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā These are the heart mantra and the near-heart mantra of the goddess.

oṃ ḍākini riṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ lāme ṝṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ khaṇḍarohe ḷṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ rūpiṇi ḹṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ khaṇḍakapāla kaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ praṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ kaṅkāla khaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ caṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ mahākaṅkāla gaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ praṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vikaṭdaṃṣṭi ghaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ maṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ suravairini ṅaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ viṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ amitāpha caṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ khaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajraprabha chaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ laṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajradeha jaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ hūṃ hūṃ hraṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ aṃkurika jhaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ aiṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajrajāṭila ñaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ maṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ mahāvira ṭaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajrahūṃkāra ṭhaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ suṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ subhadra ḍaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ śyaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajrabhadra ḍhaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ suṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ mahābhairava ṇaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ haṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ virūpākṣi taṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ khaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ mahābala thaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ caṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ ratnavajra daṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ khaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ hayagrīva dhaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ śaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ ākāśagarbha naṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ caṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ heruka paṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ suṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ padmanarteśvara phaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ maṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vairocana vaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ caṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vajrasatra bhaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ maṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ yaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ raṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ laṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ laṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ vaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ śaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ ṣaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ saṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ haṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ These are the heart secret mantras of the retinue of heroes and yoginīs, which have become the supreme of secrets; they are the very hidden mantras of the three wheels. The heroes of the wheel of mind are red, And the ḍākinīs are blue, Holding vajra and bell together, Their faces transformed like Mahābhairava's, Pervaded by aversion, in great bliss. The heroes of the wheel of speech are white, And the ḍākinīs are red, Holding lotus and bell together, Pervaded by attachment, in great bliss. The heroes of the wheel of body are yellow, And the ḍākinīs white, and the color of fire, Pervaded by delusion, in great bliss. The goddesses, the Ḍākinī and the rest, Are of various colors, great vessels. The goddesses Kākāsyā and the rest Are red, yellow, white, and green. The goddesses Yamadāhī and the rest Are blue, yellow, and green, And likewise white in color. All of them, the retinue and the rest, Are three-eyed and sit with the right leg extended. The heroes wear tiger skins, And all the goddesses are naked, Beautiful with their hair hanging loose, Twining closely with their two thighs. They wear girdles of bone ornaments, All bear garlands of skulls, And are adorned with the five mudrās. Holding skull cup and khaṭvāṅga, The heroes and the goddesses Hold the skull cup, the vajra, and the lotus, And likewise also the wheel. The goddesses, the Ḍākinī and the rest, Kākāsyā and the rest, And Yamadāhī and the rest, Hold skull cup and khaṭvāṅga, Mirror, scent, taste, and touch, A supreme and pleasing garment, And hold the ḍamaru and the bell. The Ḍākinī holds skull cup And also khaṭvāṅga, And carries bell, ḍamaru, and curved knife. The goddesses Kākāsyā and the rest Are to be fully conceived with these hand emblems. All of them sit on preta seats, Supreme in wearing the directions as their garment. The wheel of mind is fierce, The wheel of speech has agitated faces, The wheel of body is peaceful, And the Ḍākinī's faces are transformed. Having meditated just as one wishes, One should conceive the principal deity at the center: A buddha or a bodhisattva, Or any of the many various wrathful ones, Or some tathāgata, Or the special families of the mātṛkās, Marīcī, or the Inciting Goddess, And likewise Prajñāpāramitā. Whatever one meditates upon, The yoking of the mental faculty, And what is made proud by that, Is like a jewel of various forms. Or else Vajrasattva Abides among beings in various forms, All-knowing and all-accomplishing, All-pervading with great bliss. From the Highest of the Highest, the Abhidhānottara Tantra: the twenty-third chapter, teaching the meditation on the reality of the secret commitment of the yoginīs.

Then, further, I shall explain The sequence of rites of the ḍākinī families. They are explained as many and various families; In brief they are three and five, And they are also well known as six. This is the highest practice of yoga: The ḍākinīs, their retinues, and all the rest, And the three realms without remainder, Truly arise from the families. All the bodies arise each on its own; Otherwise, all are of the family of the mātṛkās, Joyful, delighted, and pleasing, Born and arising from the mind. The Blessed One, Great Delight, lord of the family of bliss, explained and taught this seed syllable of the secret heart of reality: hūṃ bruṃ āṃ hrīṃ khāṃ vāṃ. Then, at the explanation of the great mantra, The sons of all the buddhas Trembled violently and fainted, And then recalled the vajra wisdom. Akṣobhya and the family of the Eternal, Likewise Vajrasūrya, Amitābha and Amoghasiddhi, And Vajrasattva, the great bliss: In this way the bodies of the buddhas, too, Are renowned as Vajraḍāka. First is Vajraḍāka, Second is Buddhaḍāka, Third is Ratnaḍāka, Fourth is Padmaḍāka, Fifth is Viśvaḍāka, And sixth is Vajrasattva. The five are to be known as Heruka; The vajra hero is that very one as well. They partake of the food of space, And they partake of the food of recitation as well. Their emanated enjoyment is space; They partake of the food of the lotus; Their various enjoyments are emanations. Supreme and sublime of the various family, Equal to space, stainless, utterly pure, Cool like the very nature of snow, Pure in color, of supreme color, Of a color like the rays of the moon, Three-eyed, of various good fortunes, They sit upon seats of various lotuses, Abiding in the center of moon and sun, Seated in the vajra cross-legged posture. All have crowns of matted locks, And likewise garlands of skulls. All are sealed with the six mudrās, And sit well upon their own seats: Elephant, lion, and horse, Peacock, and garuḍa, And upon these, seats of pretas. If one meditates on them, they bestow accomplishment. With the vajra hand touching the earth, Holding vajra and bell at the hip, The lips of the mouth are twisted. The wheel and bell, with a swaggering air; Likewise the jewel and bell; The sequence of the lotus and bell; And the bell with the crossed vajra itself. One should meditate on all of them as haughty, The supreme and sublime vajra of the family. One should draw six maṇḍalas, Each fully endowed with four doors, Encircled by a vajra wheel, Likewise encircled by jewel and lotus, Encircled by a crossed vajra, And at the center encircled by a vajra. What is drawn as the outer maṇḍala Is a supremely pleasing multi-storied mansion. The six families are to be arranged, And there the sacred sites and the rest are arranged in sequence. What is placed at the center of the maṇḍala Is Vajrasattva, the great bliss. At its eastern door, in Pullīramalaya, are Khaṇḍakapāla and Pracaṇḍā. In the north, in Jālandhara, are Mahākaṅkāla and Caṇḍākṣī. In the west, in Oḍḍiyāna, are Kaṅkāla and Prabhāvatī. In the south, in Arbuda, are Vikaṭadaṃṣṭra and Mahānāsā. The center is the first ground, the Very Joyful. The heroes' bodies are black in color, and the ḍākinīs are the color of ash and of clouds. The heroes hold vajra and vajra bell, and the ḍākinīs hold skull cup and make the threatening gesture with the vajra. At the center of Vairocana's maṇḍala is the Blessed One Buddhaḍāka, the great bliss. At the eastern door, in Godāvarī, are Suravairin and Vīramatī. In the north, in Rāmeśvara, are Amitābha and Kharvarī. In the west, in Devīkoṭa, are Vajraprabha and Laṅkeśvarī. In the south, in Mālava, are Vajradeha and Drumacchāyā. The heroes' bodies are white, and the ḍākinīs are yellow. The Stainless ground is the center, the Buddhaḍākinī. At the center of the ground of the Luminous is Ratnaḍāka. At the eastern door, in Kāmarūpa, are Aṅkurika and Airāvatī. In the north, in Oḍra, are Vajrajaṭila and Mahābhairavā. In the west, in Triśakuni, are Mahāvīra and Vāyuvegā. In the south, in Kosala, are Vajrahūṃkāra and Surābhakṣī. The heroes are yellow, and the ḍākinīs are green. At the center of the ground of the Radiant is Padmaḍāka. At its eastern door, in Kaliṅga, are Subhadra and Śyāmādevī. In the north, in Lampāka, are Vajrabhadra and Subhadrā. In the west, in Kāñcī, are Mahābhairava and Hayakarṇā. In the south, in Himālaya, are Virūpākṣa and Khagānanā. The heroes are red, and the ḍākinīs are white. At the center of the ground of the Difficult to Master is Vajraḍāka. At its eastern door, in Pretapurī, are Mahābala and Cakravegā. In the north, in Gṛhadeva, are Vajraratna and Khaṇḍarohā. In the west, in Saurāṣṭra, are Hayagrīva and Śauṇḍinī. In the south, in Suvarṇadvīpa, are Ākāśagarbha and Cakravarmiṇī. The heroes are black, and the ḍākinīs are yellow. At the center of the ground of the Manifest is Viśvaḍāka. At its eastern door, in Nagara, are Heruka and Suvīrā. In the north, in Sindhu, are Padmanarteśvara and Mahābalā. In the west, in Maru, are Vairocana and Cakravartinī. In the south, in Kulutā, are Vajrasattva and Mahāvīryā. The heroes are multicolored, and the ḍākinīs are the color of smoke.

Outside that maṇḍala, it is encircled by a garland of hungry ghosts. Outside that, vajras all around; in the corners, draw the ḍākinīs as well: Ḍākinī and Lāmā, Khaṇḍarohā and Rūpiṇī. Draw four skull cups in the intermediate directions. In the intermediate directions of the central maṇḍala, Ḍākinī is the ground Gone Far, Lāmā the ground Unmoving, Khaṇḍarohā Good Intelligence, and Rūpiṇī the Cloud of Dharma. They are blue, yellow, and green, and the fourth is red in color. The ḍākinīs Kākāsyā and the rest are multicolored and captivating. The ḍākinīs Yamadāhī and the rest have the faces of Ardhanārīśvara, the Lord who is half woman. All the heroes stand with the right leg extended, adorned with the five mudrās, wearing tiger skins as garments, adorned with garlands of heads. Clad in the directions, with hair unbound, are the lovely families of ḍākinīs. All are seated upon hungry ghosts. Meditate on the supreme maṇḍala. The wheel that rests upon a lotus is the supreme maṇḍala of Saṃvara. They hold a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga, and a damaru in the right hand. In this way the forms of the ḍākinīs are adorned with hand emblems. The ḍākinīs Kākāsyā and the rest have the hook, the noose, and so on as emblems: in the left, the khaṭvāṅga; in the right, in their order, the noose and the chain, and likewise the bell. The ḍākinīs Yamadāhī and the rest hold the mirror and the vīṇā, scent and taste, in the right hand, and in the left a skull cup and khaṭvāṅga. Bestowing every accomplishment, crowned with the diadem of their own family, they abide upon skull cups. Having meditated on each wheel in turn, pervade it completely with its retinue. Behold the wisdom being in the heart. Through the application of all the yogas, having seen that wheel of wisdom, and having dissolved it through meditation, with the wisdom of body, speech, and mind confer empowerment, then emanate them in sequence. oṃ āḥ baṃ hūṃ With these, in their proper order, perform the control of the life breath in stages. Having emanated them evenly, bestow the empowerment. Through the application of the proper sequence, perform recitation and meditation without doubt. In accordance with the secret mantra, the one who holds the samaya should recite: oṃ śrī mahāsukha vajrastva ḍākinī jāla sambara a hūṃ phaṭ svāhā oṃ śrī buddha ḍāka a la la la a ḍākinī jālasambara hūṃ phaṭ svāhā oṃ śrī ratna ḍāka trāṃ ā ā ḍākinī jāla sambara hūṃ phaṭ svāhā oṃ śrī padma ḍāka hrīṃ hrīṃ a hūṃ ḍākinī jāla sambara hūṃ phaṭ svāhā oṃ śrī vajra ḍāka āḥ hūṃ phaṭ hūṃ ḍākinī jāla sambar hūṃ phaṭ svāhā oṃ śrī viśva ḍāka ho khaṃ daṃ ḍākinī jāl sambara hūṃ phaṭ svāhā oṃ khaṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ paṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ maṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ caṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ kaṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ praṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ baṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ maṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ suṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ byaṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ aṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ khaṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ bhaṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ laṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ baṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ traṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ eṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ maṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ maṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ baṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ caṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ suṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ śyaṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ baṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ suṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ maṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ haṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ baṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ khaṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ maṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ caṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ raṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ khaṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ haṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ śauṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ aṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ caṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ heṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ suṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ phaṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ maṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ baiṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ caṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ bhaṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ maṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ ṭaṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ laṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ khaṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ rūṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ kaṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ ūṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ śvaṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ suṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ yaṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ yaṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ yaṃ hūṃ phaṭ oṃ yaṃ hūṃ phaṭ Abiding in meditation, recite for the whole retinue and all the rest. Together with the six cakravartins, recite for the ḍākas and the ḍākinīs. Through the union of the great samaya, the samaya become the supreme samaya; through the union of the nondual embrace, if one recites the secret mantra, accomplishment will come. Certainly within six months, and not otherwise, one gains the accomplishment of the six cakravartins. Or else, Vajrasattva, Vairocana, Vajrasūrya, Padmanarteśvara the king, the lord Paramāśva, and Heruka: the wise, having meditated on them with certainty through the application of the sacred sites and so on, obtain the sequence of gradual application, the sequence of the sites of the lineage, and the commands of the yoginīs through the lineage of the guru. Otherwise the mantra practitioner wanders in saṃsāra and never sees the command.

This concludes the twenty-fourth chapter, on the command of the cakravartins of the six families, from the Abhidhānottara Tantra, the highest of the high.

Then, furthermore, I shall explain
the path of yoga, so hard to find,
the essence of the yoginīs' practice,
in accordance with the yoga of the sacred sites:
the instantaneous mode, arising from syllables.
Instantaneously, the signs are the hand emblems;
instantaneously there is Vajrasattva,
the unsurpassed great bliss.
Entering into that, and in that very manner,
instantaneously there is Heruka, glorious and supreme,
like a stainless autumn sky,
resembling the flax flower,
eight-armed and four-faced,
terrifying, and striking utter terror.
He stands with the right leg extended,
three-eyed, the lord of the yoginīs.
His left face is white, his right face red,
and his upper face is yellow and beautiful.
The hero wears a garland of skulls,
blessed by the five buddhas.
He wears a tiger skin as his garment
and is adorned with a garland of heads.
Bhairava and Kālarātri
he stands upon, pressing them down onto a lotus with his feet.
Holding vajra and bell together, he embraces his consort,
and is entwined by the thighs of Vārāhī.
Joined in equal, nondual union,
she has unbound hair and is clad in the directions,
equal and alike in the color of her body,
wearing a girdle of ornaments made of bone pieces,
with his faces and his hand emblems,
and she grants the accomplishments of yoga.
He holds a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga,
the Victor's hide above,
a hammer and a spear, and elsewhere
he is adorned with the head of Brahmā,
and holds the peaceful half moon.
His head is adorned with a crossed vajra,
his body with the six mudrās,
adorned with the marks of the vajra,
with a diadem of vajra garlands,
girdled with loincloths,
and with armlets marked with vajras.
In this way the chief of mantra practitioners
knows the secret mantra, the essence of the rite.
Vārāhī, abiding in bliss,
has set aside the Victor's hide;
from among the Victor's emblems and the rest,
Vārāhī is adorned with the damaru and the curved knife.
The Blessed One is at the center of the hub,
on a lovely eight-petaled lotus,
a multicolored lotus, beautiful in hue,
all pure, supreme, and sublime.
On the eastern petal and the other directions,
place the four, the Ḍākinī and the rest.
Place the Ḍākinī in the east,
and likewise Lāmā in the north;
in the west is Khaṇḍarohā,
and in the south is Rūpiṇī.
They are blue, yellow, and likewise red,
and green is the fourth.
Four-faced and eight-armed,
three-eyed, fierce, and majestic,
they sit in the half cross-legged posture,
lovely, with hair unbound,
clad in the directions, with garlands of heads,
adorned with the five mudrās.
They hold skull cup and khaṭvāṅga,
bell, damaru, and curved knife,
and likewise bow and arrow,
and in their hands the supreme vajra spear.
They dance with proud gestures of the hands,
intoxicated with compassion and passion.
Blue, yellow, and green,
with the upper face white;
yellow, blue, and green,
with the upper face fierce and red;
red, blue, and green,
with the upper face white and peaceful;
green, red, and blue,
with the upper face a supreme yellow.
With their feet they press down the four māras,
and with their faces they look toward the Blessed One.
In the corners are vases
filled with the nectar of wisdom,
and upon them skull cups
brimming with the milk of awakening.
Outside is the wheel of the three samayas,
with tiered chambers, beautifully adorned.
In the east is Gaurī,
